| E-58..E-63, M-01g | A fixture per slice with the item's components or the structure's state read back and nothing unearned; the Wither qualified in a staged fight before its switch; the town plan in JUnit and grown through three stages in a GameTest; the co-op month recorded with no rule broken; [design](proposals/survival-world.md). |
| M-02d..h, M-03b..e, M-04b..e | Prediction and division in JUnit; ruses with costs and chances; no strike outside a match asserted; three hunts, three races and three battles with people recorded; AI hunts, races and battles on random seeds with results; the ladders and the gallery; [design](proposals/match-modes.md). |
| E-64..E-69 | A fixture per slice with items and blocks read back and nothing unearned; the potion rules in JUnit and at their thresholds in fixtures; a village grown, a villager cured, a golem built; every advancement mapped or marked blocked with why, three earned end to end; [design](proposals/the-rest-of-the-game.md). |
| E-70a..c, F-39, F-40 | In bed at the first sleeping tick and a person's night never held; the trail's tree chosen and no leak of a hidden opponent; the Nether taken between known portals; a far lava pool sighted with its chunk never loaded and nothing below the surface ever seen; an unobserved walk with no entity and no chunk loaded mid-trip, every item identical, real before a person could see it; chunk-loads and chunk-minutes reported by the engine; [design](proposals/thrifty-travel.md). |
| E-71a..j | A twelve-block bridge with no fall, sneaking under half its ticks and 1.7 times the held crouch's pace; walks per break under 1.5 on the hideout; a stone step in 30 ticks; a zombie killed untouched in 8 of 10; a hiss behind it survived where the deaf control dies; each with its corpus scenes passing and the engine's work-cadence measures; [design](proposals/veteran-mechanics.md). |
| E-72a..e | The registry walk's unplaceable count from today's baseline to zero less a written list; a test-only block placed in every state with no code for it; a look that does not meet its face refused; each family's fixture 3 of 3; a cabin with shutters, sign, banner, hanging lantern, paired doors and a double chest exact; turning and mirroring equal to vanilla's for every state; the build suites exact and no slower; [design](proposals/block-orientation.md). |

### F-19e — The strategist's Minecraft guide and follow-ups
Depends on F-19b/F-19c/F-19d and F-25c7. A general Minecraft guide (the way to the dragon, what each larger task needs, recipes, sources, mob notice ranges, dangers, the queue) is bundled with `companion-model` and sent after the instructions, the same on every request, so it is cached. The report shows the player's queue with what it holds and the work left. A reply may add up to three follow-ups (a need, a count and the task it is for, from fixed sets), validated in core; they are kept with the habits in the player's memory of the world until met or until their task is behind it. Daily budgets count cached input at a tenth, as it is billed. A follow-up never gives anything and never orders a step: the planner does the work when it can. Acceptance: validation and transport tests, a memory round trip, live consultations that queue a fitting follow-up and nothing already held, and a live run in which follow-ups are added and worked.

### F-19c — The strategist's say in task choice
Depends on F-19b. The strategist's reply also names a focus from a fixed set (food first, wood first, none) for the same five minutes as its stance, and its note may carry a habit from a fixed set (more food carried, a second spare pickaxe for the work below, no caves, shelter at dusk) or retire one. The vocabulary is `PlanPreferences` in core, which the planner and the goal read; nothing else changes task choice. A focus puts the route's own ordinary jobs first while they still have work to do (hunting, and looking for animals by day when none is in sight; logs where a tree stands) and ends when the player is well stocked. A habit changes how much is packed or which way ore is sought (dig its own way, not caves) or when night begins, never the route; fights, shelter and healing always come first. A habit needs a note saying why; it is kept with that note in the player's memory for that world (at most four), handed to each goal it starts there, survives deaths and restarts, and goes when the note or file is removed or the strategist retires it. Accept with pure planner and validation tests, native fixtures for a focus and a habit, a memory round trip, a live consultation that sets them from a repeated problem, and live runs in which a learned habit changes play.

### E-39 — Team End expedition and Dragon skills
Depends on F-25b/c, E-04/07/11/14/15/16/17. Earn Eyes of Ender, discover the stronghold through observations, prepare portal/gear/food and traverse the End with qualified Dragon tactics. Verify readiness, permission, member authority, stop, death/recovery, restart and exact item conservation. No unqualified encounters are silently enabled.

### E-39a — Into the End
Depends on F-25c6 and F-25c7. A goal "Beat the Ender Dragon" (`DRAGON`) goes on from the End portal only where the operator has allowed the fight (`/aiplayer dragonfight on`, kept in `edits.properties`); otherwise the goal is refused and the End-portal goal stops at the portal. With the portal room found, the frame is filled with eyes it carries, one recorded placement per frame block (`native_fill_frame`), looking round the ring for the rest, and the player steps into the open portal (`native_step_in`). `EndFightPlanner` (core) names the stages from there to home. Nothing is given or placed for it. Acceptance: planner tests; a native fixture filling a ring and entering the End; refusal without the operator's leave; the full suite; then an earned run.

### E-39b — The bow and the End crystals
Depends on E-39a. `Ballistics` (core) follows vanilla 1.21.1's arrow (velocity applied, then drag 0.99 and gravity 0.05 a tick, three blocks a tick at full draw) to find the low arc to a target. `native_shoot` draws an owned bow for a full second, aims with lead for a moving target and looses one owned arrow. Crystals on the main island's pillars are shot one at a time, each from where the player stands or from a stand on end stone whose whole arc is clear of blocks, sixteen to fifty-six blocks off, away from the island's edge and the dragon's perch; a stand that misses four times, or that cannot be reached, is dropped for that crystal, and a crystal with no stand is left for a minute and tried again. Acceptance: ballistics tests; a native fixture shooting a crystal off a pillar; the full suite; a staged run that clears the island's crystals.

### E-39c — Caged crystals
Depends on E-39b. A crystal whose bars leave no clear arc from anywhere is opened: the player pillars up with its own blocks beside the pillar, breaks the bars in reach on its side (not picking them up, which would walk it off the pillar), and comes down by breaking the block underfoot one at a time (`native_break` with `underfoot`, allowed only for the block it stands on with solid ground below). Up there it does nothing else that walks; after a restart it finds its own pillar again from the blocks in the world. A barred crystal with a clear arc through broken bars counts as open, so nothing has to be remembered. Acceptance: a native fixture opening a cage and shooting the crystal; the full suite; a staged run that opens a cage.

### E-39d — The dragon, and home
Depends on E-39b/E-39c. With the crystals down (or given up on for now, so they never hold the fight up), the player keeps out from the exit portal's pillar, shoots the dragon in flight when the arc is clear, and when it perches goes to a spot in front of its head where vanilla's wings do not fling a player (each wing's box widened by four) and the head's touch does not hurt (its box widened by one, even at the top of a jump), and strikes the head in one recorded fight (`native_dragon`) with jumping critical hits, stepping back if too close and out of breath at once. With no such spot it waits for the next perch: vanilla makes a sitting dragon immune to arrows (an arrow is set alight and does nothing). Every move in the End (out of a breath cloud, out to rest, back to hold ground) picks a spot whose walk stays clear of every cloud and the perch; hurt, it rests away from the perch. When the dragon dies it goes through the exit portal, and after the credits it is brought back as a client's respawn request would bring a player back; the goal is then won. Acceptance: planner tests; native fixtures for the perch fight and the return; a staged kill and return; death and recovery in the End; then an earned run.

### E-39e — Damage dealt as well as it can be
Depends on E-39d. `DamageModel` (core) reckons damage as vanilla 1.21.1 does: each weapon's attack damage and speed, the swing's charge (`0.2 + 0.8 × charge²`), critical hits, Sharpness, the ten ticks after a hit in which a mob takes only what a larger hit adds, arrows, the mace's fall bonus, and the Ender Dragon (the head takes the full blow, other parts a quarter plus up to one; a perched dragon takes nothing from projectiles). The player fights with the held weapon that deals the most over the fight, or the hardest single blow when one hit finishes, swapped into the hotbar first, each swing at full charge; it never shoots a perched dragon and aims at the head in flight. Acceptance: pure tests of the model; a native fixture choosing the weapon from real items; a perch-fight fixture; the full suite; a staged fight on the current build.

### E-39f — Ender pearls on the arc a pearl flies
Depends on E-39b/E-39c. `Ballistics` follows thrown items as well as arrows (a pearl: 1.5 blocks a tick, drag 0.99, gravity 0.03). `PearlFlight` flies a pearl through the real blocks, puts the player where vanilla does (where the pearl struck), and finds where it comes to rest. A throw is chosen by flying it sixteen more times under vanilla's own spread (a triangular amount of up to 0.0172 on each part of its direction): it must land fourteen times in sixteen, and no throw is made when any sample, or any of the spread's eight extremes, could strike an End crystal (vanilla blows a crystal up at any hit, a pearl's too) or leave a deadly drop. `native_pearl` throws one owned pearl under the ledger and reports where the player landed. In the End a stranded player (on a pillar or a cage with no tower of its own) pearls down to end stone. Never onto a cage's roof: a roof of bars catches a pearl only about half the time, and a pearl that falls through onto the crystal blows it up beside the player; caged crystals are shot through the bars or pillared to (E-39c). Acceptance: ballistics tests; a native fixture landing a pearl on a ledge; a native fixture pearling down from a pillar beside a caged crystal; the full suite; a staged fight where the stranded pearl is used.

### E-39g — A respawn point by the End portal, and recovery after a death in the End
Depends on E-39a and F-25c7. The End fight needs a bed: three wool of one colour (from sheep) and three planks, gathered and crafted on the way like the bow and arrows. In the portal room, before going through, the player sets the bed down on two free blocks near it, never on the portal or its frame, and uses it (`native_bed`: the Overworld only, under the edit policy and a claim on both blocks, exactly one bed spent, verified once vanilla has set the respawn point there, by day or by night). Back from the End with the dragon alive, it gathers the End fight's kit again at the portal's gate with the gathering's clocks, then goes back through; the bed already down counts as the one wanted. Acceptance: planner tests; a native fixture setting the respawn point; the full suite; a staged death in the End with the gathering again and the return; then an earned run.

### E-39h — Walled in against the wings when the dragon is seen coming
Depends on E-39c. `Approach` (core) predicts when the dragon, keeping its present flight, comes within its wings' reach of the player (eleven blocks: each wing's box sits four and a half out from the body and vanilla widens it by four). Up its own tower by a cage, with the dragon in sight and due within three seconds, the player walls itself in. Vanilla's wings push a player in reach every tick, sideways and up a fifth of a block, so a wall alone would be cleared, and the guard also puts a block over the head. In order: the wall on the side the push goes toward; then the block over the head, against the pillar or the bars beside that space or on a short column of its own; then the other open sides. Each block is a recorded placement of its own blocks. It waits walled in while the dragon passes, and takes the block over its head down before going higher. Acceptance: pure tests of the prediction; a native fixture holding the player against vanilla's fling, with a control flung off a bare tower; the full suite; a staged fight where the guard is built as the dragon comes.

### E-39i — Blast exposure
Depends on E-39e. Vanilla's explosion damage, `((e² + e) / 2) × 7 × 2P + 1` with `e = (1 − d / 2P) × exposure`, where the exposure is the share of rays from the blast to points on the player's box that no block stops (`Explosion.getExposure`). A block between nearly nullifies the damage, though one point is always taken, before armour, Blast Protection and difficulty, which explosions scale with. Used to keep crystal blasts away from the player, and to shield it when it sets off a bed against the perched dragon (F-25c8). Acceptance: pure tests of the formula; a native fixture comparing predicted and real damage from a crystal blast with and without a block between; the full suite.

### E-40 — Authentic three-player playthrough
Depends on E-39 and F-23. Three AI players on at least three recorded previously untested seeds, normal survival, empty inventories, earn base/food/tools and complete the Dragon together. Record meaningful contribution by each, assignments, stock conservation, deaths/recovery and verified completion. No human gameplay interventions, grants, teleports or undiscovered-structure queries. Include restart/inference outage in the matrix, and report failures, elapsed time, interventions, tick cost and token use. No unrestricted human-equivalence claim.

### E-40a — Team runs: one to three players dividing the run
Depends on E-11, F-25c6, E-39 and F-25c8. One, two or three AI players on one run share it out as players do: while one goes to the Nether for blaze rods and pearls, another gathers iron and diamonds, food, wool for beds and arrows, and another finds the stronghold; each takes the work its kit and position suit, through E-11's assignments and reserved stock, hands over what another needs from its own earned stock (items are passed, never made), and they meet for the End fight, where crystals and the dragon are shared out. Speech describes only verified commitments and progress. Acceptance: pure tests of the division of work; native fixtures for a hand-over and a shared objective; runs with one, two and three players on the same seeds, compared for time, deaths and contribution.

### E-33a — Looking at what matters while it waits
Depends on F-10d and F-18. A native player's wait (resting to heal, by the furnace, for a sapling or daylight, a pause between steps) moves its head as a person's does: at a monster once it is in plain sight, turning quickly; at whoever speaks to it; back at a player close by who looks at it, for a moment, then back to its own business and not at that player again for ten seconds; at what it waits on; otherwise a slow look about, a few seconds each way, drawn from its own seed so players side by side do not turn as one. The head eases, never snaps: a third of what is left a tick, at most 30 degrees for a threat, 15 for a speaker, 12 for a player, 10 for its work and 5 looking about. It never looks an enderman in the eyes (vanilla's stare test with twice its margin), nor at one. Spoken to by name by a player it can see within twelve blocks, it stops between one task and the next for two seconds to look at the speaker, then goes on; never in a match, nor riding, swimming or asleep. Looking takes the wait's own lease, so a stop ends it. Acceptance: pure tests of the choice and the easing; native fixtures for a monster hidden and then seen, a furnace and a player looking at it, a word from behind a wall and from in sight, and an enderman never stared at; the full suite; a person's view of it in an ordinary client. Looks while walking, mining, placing and fighting are F-10d's.

### E-41 — Adaptive combat and PvP
The owner's design (version 0.1, 22 October 2026), kept verbatim as a [proposal](proposals/adaptive-combat-design-spec.md) and reconciled in the [combat plan](combat-plan.md). Its three targets are reported separately: mechanical competence (isolated skill trials and duels), tactical adaptation (against changing opponents, with adaptation off as the control) and resourceful survival play (unseen terrain and inventories, pursuit, outnumbered). It keeps one action owner per player (the arbiter and skill runner), observations a player could have, outcomes from the game rather than predictions, nothing replayed after cancellation, and an advisor that can neither act nor grant itself anything. Players are attacked only inside a match they joined (D7). The proposal's release gates (§16.3) are its acceptance, each claimed only after its benchmark has run.

### E-41a — Combat ruleset and adapter (Milestone 0)
Depends on E-07c and E-39e. Vanilla 1.21.1's combat written down as a ruleset in the manifest and a core class, each number with a pure test or a native fixture: the attack cooldown, critical hits, sweeping, damage immunity, knockback, an axe disabling a shield, reach and food. The body strikes within a client's reach to the hitbox and only what it can see, as the Dragon strike already does. A combat observation in core under an observation policy: own state; opponents as estimates with age and confidence (no hidden health or inventory); confirmed events kept apart from attempts. Parity fixtures for each mechanic the controller uses. Arenas that fixtures build and clear, and an encounter recorder of observations, decisions and confirmed outcomes, bounded and written off the server thread. Acceptance: the pure tests; the parity fixtures with their controls; a recorded encounter read back; the full suite.

### E-41b — Competent melee (Milestone 1)
Depends on E-41a and M-00. A duel match: two participants who joined, player targets allowed between them only while it runs; outside it the body's refusal of player targets stands. One fight skill choosing among spacing, timed strikes, strafing, a raised shield, retreat and eating to recover, by a deterministic utility with commitment and a switching margin, and never on a model. Acceptance: the mechanics suite (at least 95% completion of each skill on randomised legal starts); the baseline-duel benchmark against a declared baseline (at least 60% over 200 matches, with its uncertainty), run apart from the regression suite; no stale action after cancellation; the full suite.

### E-41g — Hit hard by a player
Depends on E-41a, E-41h and E-07c. The owner's direction (2026-10-25): a hit from another player, AI or human, dealing more than three hearts (six points of damage taken, after its armour) is lethal aggression, however the fight began. The player stops what it was doing for it: if it is equipped to defend itself and the fight is winnable (E-41h), it defends itself; otherwise it gets away (the retreat it uses for mobs, with a pearl or a pillar when it has them), blocking with a shield as it goes. It strikes back only where the rules let it strike that player at all, a match both joined (D7); anywhere else it blocks and gets away, and says to those it plays for who hit it and how hard, as it saw it. A lesser hit keeps the responses it has now. Rapport never justifies revenge: once away, or the attacker gone, it does not seek them out. Acceptance: pure tests of the threshold and the response by kit, odds and rules; native fixtures for a hard hit in a duel answered, the same hit outside a match answered by getting away with no strike back, and a lesser hit; the full suite.

### E-41h — Whether a fight is winnable
Depends on E-41a, E-39e and E-07b. The owner's direction (2026-10-25): before and during a fight, the player reckons whether it can win it, from what it can see and knows, never from an opponent's hidden state: what it deals the other a second (its weapon, timing and enchantments through the armour it can see on them, `DamageModel`, `CombatRuleset.afterArmour`); what they deal it (the weapon in their hand, the hits it has taken from them, through its own armour); the health of each (its own; theirs as believed, `HealthBeliefs`); and the means of each to heal and hold out (its food and golden apples, a shield). The odds are a margin, not a guess: how many of its hits they can take against how many of theirs it can, with a spread for what it cannot see. Winnable, it fights; even, it holds and fights only if cornered; losing, it gets away. The odds are said in plain words when it explains a choice. Acceptance: pure tests of the reckoning for kit against kit (iron against diamond, a shield, an enchanted sword, healing), of the spread, and that hidden state is never read; native fixtures for a fight taken when it is winnable and avoided when it is not; the full suite.

### E-04a — Native player 2x2 inventory crafting and crafting table creation
Depends on F-08 and F-05a/b. Craft basic 2x2 recipes (oak planks from gathered oak logs, sticks from planks, crafting table from planks) in native player inventory via `PlayerScreenHandler`. Require paid activity authority, durable `ActionAdmission` (`native_craft`) with PREPARED/DISPATCHED fencing and verified outcome, exact owned ingredient consumption, authentic vanilla recipe resolution, and complete item conservation. Reject insufficient ingredients without item loss; stop and uncertain writes settle without duplicate crafting or lost items. Test plank, stick, and crafting table recipes, insufficient ingredient refusal, and save/reload persistence in GameTests. 3x3 workstation crafting, furnace smelting, and full E-04 remain open.

### E-04b — Crafting table placement and 3x3 workstation tool crafting
Depends on E-04a, F-08, and F-05a/b. Authentically place owned blocks into the world (`native_place`) and craft 3x3 workstation recipes (`native_craft`: wooden pickaxe, wooden axe, stone tools) using a reachable placed crafting table via `CraftingScreenHandler`. Require server edit policy (`EditPolicy.Kind.PLACE`), single-use durable `ActionAdmission` (`native_place`, `native_craft`), paid activity authority, exact owned ingredient consumption, authentic vanilla recipe resolution, and complete item conservation. Reject 3x3 crafts without item loss when no crafting table is within reach or ingredients are insufficient. Autonomous progression evaluates owned wood/table to place crafting tables and craft wooden tools. Test block placement, 3x3 tool crafting, unreachable table rejection, and save/reload persistence in GameTests. Furnace smelting and full E-04 remain open.

### E-04c — Stone-tier progression and furnace crafting
Depends on E-04b, F-25c3, and F-08b. Extend native mining to stone and pickaxe-mineable blocks (`BlockTags.PICKAXE_MINEABLE`, `Blocks.STONE`, `Blocks.COBBLESTONE`) using owned pickaxes with authentic tool wear, resource drops, and durable `ActionAdmission` receipts. Expand 3x3 workstation crafting to stone tools (`stone_pickaxe`, `stone_axe`) and the furnace (`furnace` from 8 cobblestone) via `CraftingScreenHandler` with complete item conservation. Enable authentic furnace block placement (`native_place`) under server edit policy. Integrate stone-tier and furnace candidates into `PlayerRuntime.evaluateNativeCandidates` (prioritize pickaxe when mining stone, craft stone pickaxe, craft furnace, and deploy furnace). Test stone mining with pickaxe wear, stone tool crafting, furnace crafting, furnace placement, and autonomous stone progression in native GameTests. Furnace smelting and full E-04 remain open.

### E-04d — Assigned-furnace smelting and fuel accounting
Depends on E-04c, F-05a/b, and F-07a. Interact with reachable placed furnace workstations (`Blocks.FURNACE`, `AbstractFurnaceBlockEntity`) to authentically smelt ingredients into products using owned fuel and smelting inputs. Require paid activity authority, server edit policy (`EditPolicy.Kind.PLACE` / workstation interaction), and durable `ActionAdmission` (`native_smelt`) tracking. Support: (1) fuel insertion (coal, charcoal, wood planks, logs) into furnace fuel slot, (2) smeltable ingredient insertion (raw iron/copper/gold, cobblestone to stone, food/meat, logs to charcoal) into furnace input slot, (3) authentic smelting progress through vanilla furnace entity ticking, and (4) extraction of finished output items into player inventory. Enforce strict 100% item and fuel conservation (exact counts transferred, fuel burn times accounted, zero duplication, zero item deletion). Autonomous progression evaluates placed furnace proximity, owned fuel, unsmelted resources, and finished outputs to smelt charcoal/iron/food and collect products. Test fuel loading, ingredient insertion, output extraction, item conservation, and autonomous smelting progression in native GameTests.

### E-42 — Valuables
Depends on F-25c, E-08c and E-11. The owner's direction (2026-10-25): a player never walks past valuables. It understands what an item is worth to it as it stands, by what the item does for its progression from there and what it would cost to get otherwise: kit better than its own is taken and put on; what its run is short of is carried; what is of no use now but will be (gold before the Nether, an enchanted book, a saddle, rails) is taken and stored for later; what is not worth a slot is left. Worth falls off past what a player could use, and blocks of a material are worth what they are made of. Seen in passing, a drop, a structure's container or a ruined portal's gold blocks are weighed against the way to them. Only containers nobody has opened since the world made them are looted (they still hold their loot table): storage anyone has opened, a player's own or another's, is never taken from. With no room, what is worth least goes first, never what the run is short of. Acceptance: pure tests of the value model; native fixtures for a ruined portal's chest and gold blocks, a mineshaft's chest minecart, and an opened chest left alone; then the same outside the goal, the strategist told what is held and stored, and a live run reporting what was looted and what came of it.

### E-43 — Extended inventory and shared storage
Depends on E-42, E-11 and F-18. What a player has is what it holds and what it has stored: its chests, each count dated by when it last looked, so that what it plans with includes what is stored and a trip to fetch it is a step. In co-op, a teammate who puts something into shared storage or takes something out says so, from the counts before and after (F-18), and every teammate's count changes with it, through E-11's ledger. When a player looks again and something it stored is gone: in co-op it asks its team and takes the word of whoever took it, and counts again; in Manhunt or Factions, or with nobody to ask, it reckons the items lost, says only what it saw, may move what is left somewhere safer, and never accuses anyone it did not see take it, nor retaliates (rapport never justifies revenge); a player it saw take them is named to its own team only. Acceptance: pure tests of the counts, their age and the response by mode; native fixtures for a teammate's take said and counted, a chest emptied by an unseen hand found short and reported without blame, and in a match the rest moved; the full suite.

### E-44 — Raiding an opponent's storage in a match
Depends on E-42, E-43, M-00 and F-19b. The owner's direction (2026-10-25): in a match whose sides are opponents (Manhunt; Factions once it is a mode), a player that finds a chest belonging to another player asks the model whether to take that hostile action and take its valuables. A chest is an opponent's only by what the player saw: it saw them use it. The question gives the model what the player knows (the match and its side, whose chest it is, how far, opponents in sight, what it holds and its run needs) and three answers: raid it now, leave it, or watch it for later. With no model, or no usable answer within the planning lane's deadline and budgets, a local rule answers on the same facts: never with an opponent in sight, and only for what is worth the way. A raid is a recorded action as a loot is: what is worth taking comes out, exactly conserved, and the player tells its own team what it took, and only its team. Never outside a running match both joined, never a teammate's chest, never in co-op, and never against a player who is not its opponent (D7). Acceptance: pure tests of the rule, the question and the answer's checks; native fixtures for a hunter raiding a runner's chest it saw the runner use, a model's LEAVE followed, and nothing taken in co-op or from a teammate; the full suite.

### E-16a — Enchanting
Depends on E-16 and F-25b. A player enchants its weapons, armour and pickaxe as players do, at an enchanting table of its own (one in a base it built, or one it puts down) with the levels and lapis it earned: when it has thirty levels and three lapis, the best piece of each kind not yet enchanted, its sword first, and of the table's three offers the best it can pay for by what the hinted enchantment is worth on that piece. The enchanting is a recorded action: the piece the same but for what it gained, only the button's lapis and levels spent. It says what it gained. Acceptance: pure tests of what, when and which offer; a native fixture enchanting a sword at its table, and one short of the levels doing nothing; the full suite; a live run with the table and levels earned.

### E-16b — Brewing
Depends on E-16 and F-25b. A player brews the potions a run wants at a brewing stand of its own: fire resistance for the Nether, strength and slow falling for the dragon, healing for a fight, three of each. Glass bottles filled at water, made awkward with nether wart, then brewed with the potion's ingredient, the stand fuelled with blaze powder. Each fill, load and unload is a recorded action, exactly conserved. It says what it brewed. Acceptance: pure tests of what to brew and in what order; a native fixture brewing three potions of fire resistance from bottles it fills; the full suite; drinking a potion when it is wanted; a live run.

### E-16c — Netherite
Depends on E-16, E-31 and F-25b. A player upgrades its diamond kit to netherite as players do: ancient debris smelted into scrap, four scrap and four gold made into an ingot, a template copied with seven diamonds and netherrack when it has more ingots than templates, and the piece upgraded at a smithing table of its own, sword and pickaxe first, then armour, then axe. The upgrade is a recorded action: the piece its netherite self with its enchantments and name kept, one template and one ingot spent. It says what it upgraded. Acceptance: pure tests of the steps and their order; a native fixture from debris to an upgraded, still-enchanted sword; the full suite; debris mined and a template found in play.

### E-16d — Named kit
Depends on E-16a and M-01b. The owner's direction (2026-10-25): once a player has reached the enchanting stage (it has an enchanting table of its own), it names its best weapons and tools as players do: at an anvil it made, a level of its own each. A name is the model's when one is set up and the name passes the naming rules (2 to 32 letters, spaces, apostrophes and hyphens, starting with a capital, none it has used before), else its own, in keeping with its personality (`NameForge`). It says what it named. Acceptance: pure tests of the names and their rules; a native fixture naming a diamond sword and pickaxe at an anvil, a level spent each and nothing else of either changed; the full suite.

### E-45 — Villager trading
Depends on E-42, M-01c, F-10f and E-29. The owner's direction (2026-10-25): the player understands what villagers' trades are worth and how to profit by them, trades with them, and builds villager stations near its own area for the ones with good trades, bringing them there by minecart or another way. All of it earned: emeralds come from what it sells, villagers are found, never summoned, and are moved only by vanilla's means.

### E-45a — What a trade is worth
Emeralds are the currency: what each profession buys (a farmer's crops, a librarian's paper, a cleric's rotten flesh, a fletcher's sticks) is income, worth most when the player can make it renewably (its farm, sugar cane for paper); what each sells is worth what the value model (E-42) says the item is worth to the player against the emeralds it costs (a Mending book, diamond kit, ender pearls for the run). Prices move with demand and with the player's standing with the village; a trade's worth is read off the offer as the player sees it. Acceptance: pure tests of a trade's worth, of income against cost, and of what is worth buying for the run.

### E-45b — Trading
A trade is done as a player does it, at the villager's merchant screen: the offer chosen, what it costs given, what it yields taken; a recorded action, exactly conserved, the villager's stock and level respected. The first trade locks a villager to its offers. Acceptance: native fixtures for a sale and a purchase conserved, and an out-of-stock offer left.

### E-45c — Whom to keep
A villager is worth a station when its offers (or, before its first trade, its profession's) are worth more than the room and the trip. A librarian's offers are rerolled, as players do, by breaking and replacing its lectern before its first trade, until it offers a book worth keeping or the player gives up. Acceptance: pure tests of the choice; a native fixture for a lectern reroll to a wanted book.

### E-45d — The trading hall
Near its base (M-01c), on ground it picked, a hall of cells: each with the villager's job block, a bed within reach, a door or a slab the villager cannot path out of, lit and walled against zombies; built through the needs queue as its other builds are (E-29, E-08c). Acceptance: a native fixture for a hall built and a villager in a cell keeping its profession.

### E-45e — Bringing a villager home
The cheapest way the player's kit and the ground allow: a boat over water (a villager pushed into it, rowed home), a minecart on rails it lays from the villager to the hall (iron and sticks of its own; powered rails where it has gold and redstone), or on foot over short, safe ground. Into its cell and the way closed behind it. Never by commands, leads or anything vanilla does not allow. Acceptance: native fixtures for a villager brought by boat and by minecart and settled in its cell; a live run reporting the trades it then made.

## Game-mode cards
### M-00 — Game-mode and match framework
Depends on F-25b/F-25c. Owner/operator selects a mode per world or match from the K panel or `/aiplayer mode`; the existing playstyle profile maps onto it rather than running beside it. A mode declares its rules: survival or creative, edit policy, PvP among opted-in participants (D7), announced mode items (D6), respawn rules and win conditions. Matches have a lobby, roles, countdown, timer, end and results. Presentation: titles, boss bar, action bar, scoreboard, sounds and particles, visible without the client mod, with richer screens when it is installed. Acceptance: native fixtures for every lifecycle transition and rule; live runs with ordinary clients; stopping a match revokes all match-granted rules synchronously.

### M-01 — Co-op Survival mode
Depends on M-00, F-25c4, E-07, E-13, E-16, E-39, M-05. A regular playthrough with natural progression (owner, 2026-10-23): a house or base, crop farms, a ranch with animal pens and breeding, horses tamed, saddled and ridden, XP farms and enchanting, all from earned materials. Progress tracks the human: the AI player does not run ahead of the group's milestones. Boss fights are an optional progression, set per world: off, it prepares gear and supplies for a boss but starts one only when a human does, and then fights alongside; on, it works toward the bosses as part of the playthrough. It builds chosen schematics by computing the bill of materials, gathering or crafting every block, and placing them in a buildable order. Needs are a queue (owner, 2026-10-23): it builds what it can from what it holds, fills in what it has, and fetches the shortfall together in one planned trip; an item urgent to finishing, or easy to get from a known chest, it fetches at once. Bases improve in three stages (owner, 2026-10-23): a small base to start the world and store resources, then bigger and better, ending in detailed, multi-floor, functional builds (rooms with a purpose, storage, workstations, an enchanting room). Farms and other functional builds are staged the same way; a mob-spawner farm is a one-off functional build where a spawner is found. Acceptance: live sessions in which a human sets the pace, nothing is granted, and a schematic build completes from earned blocks.

### M-02 — Manhunt mode
Depends on M-00, M-03 and native PvP combat. Roles: runner or hunter, with humans and AI players in any mix, including AI against AI. Hunters track the runner with the announced compass rule (D6), the same for humans and AIs, pointing at the runner's last portal across dimensions. Everyone starts from scratch (owner, 2026-10-23). The hunter doctrine is iron gear and a shield, then pursuit, gearing up further as the game goes on and going further only when it is opportune. The runner's goal is to beat the game quickly but reliably, not at speedrun pace, while evading the hunters: keep distance, block pursuit, and fight only when cornered. Win conditions are dragon dead or runner dead; hunters respawn. Acceptance: matches of AI against human and AI against AI that run to a result with no shortcuts.

### M-03 — Speedrun mode
Depends on F-25c4, E-15, Nether and End skills, E-39. A random seed, with no seed knowledge or locator (the seed locator was removed). Any% glitchless along community-accepted routes: lava-pool or ruined-portal entry, bastion bartering for pearls, fortress blaze rods, eye-of-ender triangulation, and bed or one-cycle dragon kills. An in-game timer and splits. Acceptance: completed runs on several random seeds with recorded times and no rule-breaking actions.

### M-04 — Builder mode
Depends on M-00, E-29 and M-05; optionally F-19 for theme interpretation. A generated arena with a lobby, protected plots, and spectator and voting paths. Each round has a random theme announced with a title, a timed build in creative mode (blocks are free in this mode only), voting by human and AI players, then results with a podium and fireworks. AI builders turn the theme into a structured build plan, using the local design grammar, reference schematics from M-05, and optionally the model as planner through companion-model (data plans only, no generated code), then place it block by block within the time limit. Acceptance: complete 1v1 rounds against a human and between AIs, with ordinary-client presentation.

### M-06 — Factions mode (optional)
Depends on M-00, M-01's progression and E-41's combat. The owner's optional mode (2026-10-23): the natural survival progression of M-01, but each AI player plays as an independent faction, unfriendly to every other player and AI player in the match. It plays for strategy as well as progress: a hidden bunker as a base, concealed resource farms, defences, scouting others' bases, and choosing when to fight. A human is fair game only after joining the match (D7); a player outside it is never attacked. Entering another participant's base or taking from its chests follows the mode's announced rules and the server's protection, the same for humans and AIs; nothing is free. Acceptance: live sessions in which AI players and a joined human each build up in secret and contest one another, with no shortcuts and no harm to anyone who did not join.

### M-05 — Schematic library
Depends on E-08. Import Sponge `.schem`, Litematica `.litematic` and vanilla structure `.nbt` files, bundled with the mod or supplied by users (D8). Store the source link, creator and license with each import. Compute a bill of materials and a buildable placement order (supports first, gravity blocks last), and report blocks that survival cannot obtain. Acceptance: pure parser and ordering tests; a native fixture that builds a small imported structure from owned blocks.
